Braided cohomology of quasi-triangular bialgebras and braided Morita invariance
Shota Inoue, Ayako Itaba

Abstract
. We introduce the braided cochain complex and the braided cohomology of braided coalgebras in linear monoidal categories, and compare the braided cohomology of braided coalgebras living in different linear monoidal categories using relative morphisms. The symmetric cohomology was introduced for groups by Staic, and was generalized to cocommutative Hopf algebras by Shiba, Sanada, and the second author. This cohomology involves degreewise actions of the symmetric groups on a cochain complex, which come from the usual symmetric monoidal structure on the category of modules. We generalize this framework by dealing with arbitrary linear monoidal categories, and by replacing symmetries with braidings defined merely on an object. We first give a convenient description of relative morphisms, and apply this result to prove that the braided cochain complex of quasi-triangular bialgebras is a braided Morita invariant under a certain condition, which is automatically satisfied in the finite-dimensional case.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
18G90, 16D90, 16T10, 18C40, 18M15, 18N50.

1.
Introduction

Staic
[
38
]
defined the notion of the symmetric cohomology of groups, motivated from lattice topological field theory.
Staic
[
39
]
showed that the second symmetric cohomology of a group classifies equivalence classes of extensions which possess a suitable (set-theoretic) section.
Pirashvili
[
31
]
showed that the third symmetric cohomology of a group classifies equivalence classes of crossed extensions subject to similar conditions, when the group has no elements of order two.
These results have familiar counterparts for the classical cohomology of groups.
Pirashvili
[
30
]
related the symmetric cohomology of groups with the exterior cohomology of groups, introduced by Zarelua
[
44
]
, by establishing a section from the latter to the former, which is shown to be an isomorphism for degrees less than or equal to four.
Pirashvili
[
30
]
also constructed a spectral sequence such that the exterior cohomology groups appear on the second page, and it gives a five-term exact sequence which relates the exterior cohomology with the classical cohomology of groups.
We refer the reader to Bardakov–Neshchadim–Singh
[
9
]
, Pirashvili–Pirashvili
[
32
]
, and Todea
[
41
]
for related work.
One common way to obtain a reasonable generalization of concepts in the theory of groups (and Lie algebras) is to search for their analogs in the context of cocommutative Hopf algebras, by dealing with group algebras (and universal enveloping algebras).
Shiba–Sanada–Itaba
[
36
]
generalized the definition of the symmetric cohomology to this setting.
They associated to cocommutative Hopf algebras some cochain complexes equipped with degreewise actions of the symmetric groups, and showed that they are isomorphic in the equivariant sense.
By taking the submodules of invariants with respect to the degreewise actions, they defined the subcomplexes called the non-homogeneous and homogeneous cochain complexes, where the symmetric cohomology appears as the cohomology of these chain complexes.
This generalizes the constructions described as in
[
38
]
and
[
30
]
.
In the same work, the authors also defined the notion of the symmetric Hochschild cohomology of cocommutative Hopf algebras, and they constructed an explicit isomorphism between the symmetric cohomology and the symmetric Hochschild cohomology of cocommutative Hopf algebras, which can be thought of as a symmetric version of the Eckmann-Shapiro type isomorphism between the Hopf algebra cohomology and the Hochschild cohomology of Hopf algebras (see Pevtsova–Witherspoon
[
29
, Lemma 12]
for a precise statement).
Note that the homogeneous cochain complex can be defined in a slightly more general setting of cocommutative bialgebras.

The category of (co)modules over a bialgebra has a natural structure of a linear monoidal category, and parallel to the classical Morita theory for algebras, bialgebras are often studied and are classified up to monoidal Morita(-Takeuchi) equivalence; that is, up to linear monoidal equivalence of their (co)module categories.
Equivalences between module categories are classified by bialgebra twists, and those between comodule categories by bi-Galois objects.
Ng–Schauenburg
[
27
]
showed that the (higher) Frobenius-Schur indicators of
complex semisimple quasi-Hopf algebras are stable under bialgebra twists.
Bichon
[
10
]
showed that the cohomological dimension of Hopf algebras over an algebraically closed field is a monoidally Morita-Takeuchi invariant, in the sense that it is preserved by equivalences between the comodule categories, under various homological
assumptions on Hopf algebras. Zhu
[
45
]
showed that the injective dimension of Artin-Schelter Gorenstein Hopf algebras of type
\mathbf{FP}_{\infty}
over a field is a monoidally Morita-Takeuchi invariant, and that the global dimension of Hopf algebras is not, by giving a counterexample of (noetherian affine PI) Hopf algebras.
Kaoutit–Kowalzig
[
21
]
showed that the Hopf-cyclic (co)homology of left Hopf algebroids is stable under Morita base changes.
Farinati
[
17
]
showed that several cohomology theories of (positively graded) differential graded (or dg, for short) coalgebras over a field are stable under equivalences between the derived categories of dg comodules satisfying mild hypotheses; for example, Hochschild (co)homology of (concentrated) coalgebras is stable under derived equivalences induced from a cotilting bicomodule.

Braidings on the category of (co)modules over a bialgebra correspond to (universal) R-matrices (and to R-forms), and a bialgebra equipped with an R-matrix (or an R-form) is called a (co)quasi-triangular bialgebra.
Quasi-triangular bialgebras are often viewed as generalizations of cocommutative bialgebras, and it seems appropriate to study them up to braided Morita equivalence; that is, up to linear braided monoidal equivalence of their module categories.
Shimizu
[
37
]
proved the braided Morita invariance of some representations of the braid groups (of Coxeter type A) associated to quasi-triangular Hopf algebras over a field.
For semisimple Hopf algebras over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, these monoidal Morita invariants are related to the Reshetikhin-Turaev invariants of closed
3
-manifolds.
This result of
[
37
]
was
extended by Müller–Walton
[
26
]
, where they established an invariance result for quasi-triangular coideal subalgebras of a finite-dimensional quasi-triangular Hopf algebra, using representations of the braid groups of Coxeter types BC or D instead.

The construction of the (non-)homogeneous cochain complex, mentioned as above, involves degreewise actions of the symmetric groups.
In the homogeneous case, these degreewise actions are induced from the usual symmetry on the monoidal category of modules, which consist of flip morphisms.
It is natural to extend this notion to the setting of quasi-triangular bialgebras, by replacing symmetric groups with braid groups, and the evident symmetry with arbitrary braidings.
Moreover, since the aforementioned actions of the symmetric groups make use of only the component of the symmetry at the regular module, a slightly more appropriate candidate for this cohomology theory is the notion of a braided bialgebra (in the sense of Takeuchi
[
40
]
).
We take a step further here, and define the braided cohomology which takes as input braided coalgebras, introduced by Ardizzoni–Menini
[
4
]
, in linear monoidal categories.
More precisely, the braided cohomology is defined to be the cohomology of a cochain complex, called the braided cochain complex of a braided coalgebra, generalizing the notion of the homogeneous cochain complex in the previous setting.
Working in this generality allows us to consider the braided cohomology of (not necessarily cocommutative) quasi-triangular bialgebras, for example, the Sweedler Hopf algebra, the Kac-Paljutkin Hopf algebra, and the Drinfel’d doubles of finite-dimensional Hopf algebras over a field.
Meanwhile, this framework enables us to study braided coalgebras in the (differential) graded setting, or those in the monoidal category of Yetter-Drinfel’d modules over a Hopf algebra with
bijective antipode.
We also give various constructions and results which are applicable in non-additive situations, for example, when one works in the monoidal category of simplicial sets, or that of species, or when one deals with the (symmetric) monoidal category of symmetric, orthogonal, or Elmendorf-Kriz-Mandell-May spectra, with a view toward applications to combinatorics or stable homotopy theory.

Our constructions of the braided cochain complex and of the braided cohomology involve (monoidal) functors from the augmented braided category
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
and its variant
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}
.
The augmented braided category is an example of (the augmented version of) crossed simplicial groups, introduced independently by Krasauskas
[
23
]
and Fiedorowicz–Loday
[
18
]
.
This notion includes the cyclic and symmetric category, which are related to the homology theories called the cyclic and symmetric homology, and also to their homotopical analogs called the topological Hochschild and symmetric homology.
For example, Connes
[
13
]
introduced the cyclic category to have a homological interpretation of the cyclic (co)homology, which was also used in Nikolaus–Scholze
[
28
]
to understand the topological cyclic homology in terms of the cyclotomic spectrum structure on the topological Hochschild homology.
We refer to
[
18
]
for a unified treatment of (co)homology theories associated to crossed simplicial groups, and to Angelini-Knoll–Merling–Péroux
[
3
]
for their
\infty
-categorical refinement called the topological homology. Our work reveals a connection between the (augmented) braided category and the braided cohomology of braided coalgebras, providing an answer to Problem 22 by Singh in Bardakov–Gongopadhyay–Singh–Vesnin–Wu
[
8
]
.
We expect that the braided cohomology admits an
\infty
-categorical generalization.
We note that the topological braid homology was introduced in Angelini-Knoll–Chan–Gerhardt–Merling–Péroux
[
2
]
, where they identified (the nerve of) the augmented braided category with the
\mathbb{E}_{2}
-monoidal envelope of the associative operad.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category,
(C,c)
a braided coalgebra in
\mathcal{C}
(see Definition
3.1
), and
M
an object in
\mathcal{C}
.
We associate to
(C,c)
a strict monoidal functor
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}_{\bullet}(C,c)\colon{(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}}\to{[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]}
, where
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
is the (large) strict monoidal category of endofunctors of
\mathcal{C}
.
This functor plays a crucial role in the construction of the braided cochain complex
\mathrm{KB}^{\bullet}(C,c,M)
of
(C,c)
with coefficients in
M
, and also in the study of relative morphisms (see Definition
4.2
).
This s a relative version of monoidal natural transformations between strict monoidal functors of the form
{\mathcal{I}^{\hskip 0.40974pt\mathrm{op}}}\to{[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]}
, where
\mathcal{I}
denotes either
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
or
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}
, and
\mathcal{C}
varies over any monoidal categories.
Our first main result describes relative morphisms between strict monoidal functors of the form
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}_{\bullet}(C,c)
, in terms of the structural morphisms of braided coalgebras.

Theorem A

(Theorem
4.12
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be monoidal categories,
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon,c)
and
(D,\Delta^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime},c^{\prime})
braided coalgebras in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, respectively, and
(F,\varphi_{2},\varphi_{0})\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
a monoidal functor.
Then, the assignment

\eta_{\bullet}^{\vphantom{\prime\theta}}\mapsto r^{\prime}_{F(C)}\circ(F(C)\varotimes\varphi_{0}^{-1})\circ(\varphi_{2})_{C,I}^{-1}\circ(\eta_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{\vphantom{\prime\theta}})_{I}\circ(D\varotimes\varphi_{0})\circ r_{D}^{\prime{\hskip 0.40974pt}-1}

induces a bijective correspondence between

•

collections
\eta_{\bullet}^{\vphantom{\prime\theta}}=\{\eta_{n\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{\vphantom{\prime\theta}}\colon{{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}_{n}(D,c^{\prime})\circ F}\to{F\circ{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}_{n}(C,c)}\}_{n\geq 0}
of natural transformations such that the pair
(F,\eta_{\bullet}^{\vphantom{\prime\theta}})
is a relative morphism from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}_{\bullet}(C,c)
to
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}_{\bullet}(D,c^{\prime})
, and
\eta_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{\vphantom{\prime\theta}}=(\varphi_{2})_{C,-}\circ(\alpha^{\prime}\varotimes F(-))
for some morphism
\alpha^{\prime}\colon{D}\to{F(C)}
in
\mathcal{D}
, and

•

morphisms
\alpha\colon{D}\to{F(C)}
in
\mathcal{D}
which satisfies the following equalities
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}=F(\varepsilon)\circ\alpha;\qquad(\varphi_{2})_{C,C}\circ(\alpha\varotimes\alpha)\circ\Delta^{\prime}

\displaystyle=F(\Delta)\circ\alpha;

\displaystyle(\varphi_{2})_{C,C}\circ(\alpha\varotimes\alpha)\circ c^{\prime}

\displaystyle=F(c)\circ(\varphi_{2})_{C,C}\circ(\alpha\varotimes\alpha).

We apply this result to illustrate the utility of the braided cochain complex and the braided cohomology for studying quasi-triangular
\Bbbk
-bialgebras.
Since every quasi-triangular
\Bbbk
-bialgebra
(A,R)
gives rise to a braided coalgebra
(A,{c}^{R}_{A,A})
in the
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category
{\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}\mathcal{M}}
of
A
-modules, where
{c}^{R}
denotes the corresponding braiding on
{\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}\mathcal{M}}
, we can transfer the constructions and properties established for braided coalgebras in general to this setting in a straightforward manner.
In particular, we write
\mathrm{KB}^{\bullet}(A,R,M)
for the braided cochain complex
\mathrm{KB}^{\bullet}(A,{c}^{R}_{A,A},M)
of
(A,{c}^{R}_{A,A})
with coefficients in an
A
-modules
M
.
Our next main result asserts that, under mild hypotheses, the braided Morita equivalence classes of quasi-triangular
\Bbbk
-bialgebras determine their braided cochain complexes up to isomorphism.

Theorem B

(Theorem
4.24
)
.

Let
(A,R)
and
(B,R^{\prime})
be quasi-triangular
\Bbbk
-bialgebras, and suppose that
F\colon{({\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}\mathcal{M}},{c}^{R})}\to{({\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 6.883pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{B}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.883pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{B}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.91846pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{B}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.91846pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{B}}}\mathcal{M}},{c}^{R^{\prime}})}
is a
\Bbbk
-linear braided monoidal equivalence such that
U_{B}\circ F\cong U_{A}
as
\Bbbk
-linear functors, where
U_{A}
and
U_{B}
denote the forgetful functors, defined as in
Notation
4.17

(1)
.
Then, there exists an isomorphism
\mathrm{KB}^{\bullet}(A,R,M)\cong\mathrm{KB}^{\bullet}(B,R^{\prime},F(M))
for all
A
-modules
M
.
In particular, the braided cohomology of quasi-triangular
\Bbbk
-bialgebras is invariant under braided Morita equivalences which commute with the forgetful functors up to
\Bbbk
-linear natural isomorphism.

Our last main result asserts that the braided cochain complex of a finite-dimensional quasi-triangular bialgebra over a field
\Bbbk
is a braided Morita invariant.
We deduce this result from Theorem
4.24
by virtue of a discussion of the interaction between filtered colimits and tensor products (see Lemma
4.26
).
Given a finite-dimensional
\Bbbk
-bialgebra
A
, we let
{\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}\mathcal{M}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\textnormal{fin}}
denote the (monoidal) full subcategory of
{\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}\mathcal{M}}
consisting of finitely generated
A
-modules (see Notation
4.17

(2)
).

Corollary C

(Corollary
4.25
)
.

Let
(A,R)
and
(B,R^{\prime})
be finite-dimensional quasi-triangular bialgebras over a field
\Bbbk
, and suppose that
F\colon{({\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.51392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.68063pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{A}}}\mathcal{M}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\textnormal{fin}},{c}^{R})}\to{({\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 6.883pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{B}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.883pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{B}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.91846pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{B}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.91846pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{B}}}\mathcal{M}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\textnormal{fin}},{c}^{R^{\prime}})}
is a
\Bbbk
-linear braided monoidal equivalence.
Then, there exists an isomorphism
\mathrm{KB}^{\bullet}(A,R,M)\cong\mathrm{KB}^{\bullet}(B,R^{\prime},F(M))
for all finitely generated
A
-modules
M
.
In particular, the braided cohomology of finite-dimensional quasi-triangular bialgebras over a field is a braided Morita invariant.

Finally, we provide an example by comparing the
\Bbbk
-vector spaces
\mathrm{KB}^{1}(A,R_{\lambda},M)
, in the case when
A
is the Sweedler Hopf algebra, whose R-matrices are known to be parameterized by
\lambda\in\Bbbk
.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we review some concepts from the theory of (braided) monoidal categories and of
\Bbbk
-linear categories.
We also introduce the augmented braided category and its variant, which appear in this paper as the domains of (monoidal) functors which play a crucial role in the construction of the braided cohomology.
In Section
3
, we construct strict monoidal functors from braided coalgebras in monoidal categories (see Proposition
3.13
), and based on this construction, we introduce in Subsection
3.2
the braided cochain complex and the braided cohomology of braided coalgebras in
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal categories.
In Section
4
, we introduce and study the notion of a relative morphism, and we show that relative morphisms in a particular setting are essentially the same as a relative version of morphisms of coalgebras (see Theorem
4.12
).
We apply this to prove that the braided cochain complex of quasi-triangular
\Bbbk
-bialgebras is a braided Morita invariant under some hypotheses (see Theorem
4.24
), which become automatic in the finite-dimensional case (see Corollary
4.25
).

Convention
.

•

Throughout this paper, we fix a commutative ring
\Bbbk
.

•

We refer to locally small
1
-categories simply as categories, and covariant functors as functors.

•

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
and
X\in\mathcal{C}
, we write
\mathrm{Aut}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{C}}(X)
for the group of automorphisms of
X
in
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, we let
\mathcal{C}^{\hskip 0.57367pt\mathrm{op}}
denote the opposite category of
\mathcal{C}
, which is small if
\mathcal{C}
is.

•

For categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, we let
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}]
denote the (large) category of functors from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
.

•

We use the symbol
\star
for the horizontal composition of natural transformations.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we recall some basic definitions and preliminary results from the theory of (braided) monoidal categories; we refer to Kassel
[
22
, Chapters XI and XII]
for details.
Then, we introduce two (small) strict monoidal categories, the augmented braided category as well as its variant (see Definitions
2.6
and
2.7
), which play a significant role in this paper.
We also collect elementary facts on evaluations and on
\Bbbk
-linear categories, which will be used in an argument after Definition
4.1
.

2.1.
(Braided) monoidal categories and (braided) monoidal functors

We recall that a
monoidal category
is a triple
(\mathcal{C},\mathord{\varotimes},I)
consisting of a category
\mathcal{C}
, a functor
\mathord{\varotimes}\colon{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
, an object
I
in
\mathcal{C}
, equipped with fixed collections
a=\{a_{X,Y,Z}\colon{(X\varotimes Y)\varotimes Z}\to{X\varotimes(Y\varotimes Z)}\}_{X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}}
,
l=\{l_{X}\colon{I\varotimes X}\to{X}\}_{X\in\mathcal{C}}
, and
r=\{r_{X}\colon{X\varotimes I}\to{X}\}_{X\in\mathcal{C}}
of isomorphisms in
\mathcal{C}
, subject to the naturality of
a
,
l
, and
r
and certain coherence conditions between them (see
[
22
, Definition XI.2.1]
for a precise definition).
In this case, we often refer to the pair
(\mathord{\varotimes},I)
as a
monoidal structure
on
\mathcal{C}
.
A monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\mathord{\varotimes},I)
is said to be
strict
if the equality
(X\varotimes Y)\varotimes Z=X\varotimes(Y\varotimes Z)
holds for all
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}
, the equality
I\varotimes X=X=X\varotimes I
holds for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
, and the morphisms
a_{X,Y,Z}
,
l_{X}
, and
r_{X}
are the identities.

We will often abbreviate a monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\mathord{\varotimes},I)
to
\mathcal{C}
.
We will frequently distinguish structural collections of isomorphisms by writing them either as
a
,
l
,
r
, or as
a^{\prime}
,
l^{\prime}
,
r^{\prime}
.

Definition 2.1

(cf.
[
22
, Definition XI.4.1 (a)]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be monoidal categories.
A
monoidal functor
from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
is a triple
(F,\varphi_{2},\varphi_{0})
consisting of

•

a functor
F\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
,

•

an isomorphism
\varphi_{0}\colon{I}\to{F(I)}
in
\mathcal{D}
, and

•

an isomorphism
(\varphi_{2})_{X,Y}\colon{F(X)\varotimes F(Y)}\to{F(X\varotimes Y)}
in
\mathcal{D}
for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
,

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

For all pairs of morphisms
f\colon{X}\to{X^{\prime}}
and
g\colon{Y}\to{Y^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have the equality

(2.1)

(\varphi_{2})_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}}\circ(F(f)\varotimes F(g))=F(f\varotimes g)\circ(\varphi_{2})_{X,Y}.

(2)

For all
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}
, we have the equality

(2.2)

F(a_{X,Y,Z})\circ(\varphi_{2})_{X\varotimes Y,Z}\circ((\varphi_{2})_{X,Y}\varotimes F(Z))=(\varphi_{2})_{X,Y\varotimes Z}\circ(F(X)\varotimes(\varphi_{2})_{Y,Z})\circ a^{\prime}_{F(X),F(Y),F(Z)}.

(3)

For all
X\in\mathcal{C}
, we have the equalities

(2.3)

\displaystyle l^{\prime}_{F(X)}

\displaystyle=F(l_{X})\circ(\varphi_{2})_{I,X}\circ(\varphi_{0}\varotimes F(X));

(2.4)

\displaystyle r^{\prime}_{F(X)}

\displaystyle=F(r_{X})\circ(\varphi_{2})_{X,I}\circ(F(X)\varotimes\varphi_{0}).

In this case, we often refer to the pair
(\varphi_{2},\varphi_{0})
as a
monoidal structure
on
F
.
When
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are strict, a monoidal functor
(F,\varphi_{2},\varphi_{0})
from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D}
is said to be
strict
if the equality
F(X)\varotimes F(Y)=F(X\varotimes Y)
holds for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
, the equality
I=F(I)
holds, and the morphisms
(\varphi_{2})_{X,Y}
and
\varphi_{0}
are the identities.
Given such a functor
F\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
, condition
(1)
holds if and only if the equality
F(f)\varotimes F(g)=F(f\varotimes g)
holds for all pairs of morphisms
f\colon{X}\to{X^{\prime}}
and
g\colon{Y}\to{Y^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{C}
, whereas conditions
(2)
and
(3)
become automatic (see also Johnson–Yau
[
20
, Proposition 4.1.8]
).

We will often abbreviate a monoidal functor
(F,\varphi_{2},\varphi_{0})
to
F
.
We note that some authors do not require
(\varphi_{2})_{X,Y}
and
\varphi_{0}
of a monoidal functor to be isomorphisms, and they refer to a monoidal functor in our sense as a
strong
monoidal functor (see
[
20
, page 12]
).

Definition 2.2

(cf.
[
22
, Definition XI.4.1 (b)]
)
.

Let
(F,\varphi_{2},\varphi_{0}),(G,\varphi_{2}^{\prime},\varphi_{0}^{\prime})\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
be monoidal functors.
A
monoidal natural transformation
from
(F,\varphi_{2},\varphi_{0})
to
(G,\varphi_{2}^{\prime},\varphi_{0}^{\prime})
is a natural transformation
\eta\colon{F}\to{G}
such that the equalities
\eta_{I}\circ\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{0}^{\prime}
and
\eta_{X\varotimes Y}\circ(\varphi_{2})_{X,Y}=(\varphi_{2}^{\prime})_{X,Y}\circ(\eta_{X}\varotimes\eta_{Y})
hold for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
(F,\varphi_{2},\varphi_{0})\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
be a monoidal functor.
Then, the assignment
V\mapsto V\varotimes F(-)
gives a faithful functor
{\mathcal{D}}\to{[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}]}
.

Proof.

This functor is defined by postcomposing
F
with the functor
{\mathcal{D}}\to{[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}]};V\mapsto V\varotimes(-)
, and the faithfulness can be verified using the equalities

\displaystyle r^{\prime}_{W}\circ(W\varotimes\varphi_{0}^{-1})\circ(g\varotimes F(-))_{I}\circ(V\varotimes\varphi_{0})\circ r_{V}^{\prime{\hskip 0.40974pt}-1}

\displaystyle=r^{\prime}_{W}\circ(W\varotimes\varphi_{0}^{-1})\circ(g\varotimes F(I))\circ(V\varotimes\varphi_{0})\circ r_{V}^{\prime{\hskip 0.40974pt}-1}

\displaystyle=r^{\prime}_{W}\circ(g\varotimes I)\circ r_{V}^{\prime{\hskip 0.40974pt}-1}=g

for all morphisms
g\colon{V}\to{W}
in
\mathcal{D}
, where the third equality follows from the naturality of
r^{\prime}
.
∎

Definition 2.4

(cf.
[
22
, Definition XIII.1.1]
)
.

A
braided monoidal category
is a pair
(\mathcal{C},c)
consisting of

•

a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, and

•

an isomorphism
{c}_{X,Y}\colon{X\varotimes Y}\to{Y\varotimes X}
in
\mathcal{C}
for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
,

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

For all pairs of morphisms
f\colon{X}\to{X^{\prime}}
,
g\colon{Y}\to{Y^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have the equality

(2.5)

{c}_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}}\circ(f\varotimes g)=(g\varotimes f)\circ{c}_{X,Y}.

(2)

For all
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}
, we have the equalities

(2.6)

\displaystyle(Y\varotimes{c}_{X,Z})\circ a_{Y,X,Z}\circ({c}_{X,Y}\varotimes Z)

\displaystyle=a_{Y,Z,X}\circ{c}_{X,Y\varotimes Z}\circ a_{X,Y,Z};

(2.7)

\displaystyle({c}_{X,Z}\varotimes Y)\circ a_{X,Z,Y}^{-1}\circ(X\varotimes{c}_{Y,Z})

\displaystyle=a_{Z,X,Y}^{-1}\circ{c}_{X\varotimes Y,Z}\circ a_{X,Y,Z}^{-1}.

In this case, we refer to the collection
c=\{{c}_{X,Y}\}_{X,Y\in\mathcal{C}}
as a
braiding
on
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 2.5

(cf.
[
22
, Definition XIII.3.6]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},c)
and
(\mathcal{D},c^{\prime})
be braided monoidal categories.
A
braided monoidal functor
from
(\mathcal{C},c)
to
(\mathcal{D},c^{\prime})
is a monoidal functor
(F,\varphi_{2},\varphi_{0})\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
such that the following equality holds for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
:

(2.8)

(\varphi_{2})_{Y,X}\circ{c^{\prime}}_{F(X),F(Y)}=F({c}_{X,Y})\circ(\varphi_{2})_{X,Y}.

2.2.
The augmented braided category

Recall that for
n\geq 0
, the
braid group
B_{n}
with
n
strands
was introduced by Artin
[
5
]
as the group of ambient isotopy classes of braids with
n
strands.
This group is isomorphic to the fundamental group of the configuration space of unordered distinct
n
points in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
For our purpose, it suffices to recall only the following presentation of the braid groups; for all
n\geq 0
, the group
B_{n+1}
is generated by
\{g_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
, subject to the relations
g_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}g_{j\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=g_{j\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}g_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
with
\lvert i-j\rvert\geq 2
and
g_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}g_{i+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}g_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=g_{i+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}g_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}g_{i+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
for all
1\leq i<n
(see also
[
22
, Corollary X.6.6]
).

Definition 2.6

(cf.
[
2
, Definition 4.2.1]
)
.

The
augmented braided category

(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
is the category, with the objects
\langle n\rangle
for
n\geq 0
, and morphisms generated by
\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}\colon{\langle n\rangle}\to{\langle n+1\rangle}
for
0\leq j\leq n
,
\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}\colon{\langle n+2\rangle}\to{\langle n+1\rangle}
for
0\leq j\leq n
, and
\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}},(\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})^{-1}\colon{\langle n+1\rangle}\to{\langle n+1\rangle}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, subject to the following conditions:

(1)

For all
0\leq i<j\leq n
, we have the equality
\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\delta_{i\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n-1\vphantom{nm}}=\delta_{i\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\delta_{j-1\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n-1\vphantom{nm}}
.

(2)

For all
0\leq i\leq j\leq n
, we have the equality
\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\sigma_{i\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n+1\vphantom{nm}}=\sigma_{i\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\sigma_{j+1\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n+1\vphantom{nm}}
.

(3)

For all
0\leq i\leq n+1
and
0\leq j\leq n
, we have the following equality:

\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\delta_{i\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n+1\vphantom{nm}}=\begin{cases}\delta_{i\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\sigma_{j-1\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n-1\vphantom{nm}}&if $i<j$,\\
\mathrm{id}_{\langle n+1\rangle}&if $i=j$ or $i=j+1$,\\
\delta_{i-1\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n-1\vphantom{nm}}&if $i>j+1$.\end{cases}

(4)

For all
1\leq i\leq n
, the morphism
\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
is an isomorphism in
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
, whose inverse is given by
(\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})^{-1}
.

(5)

For all
1\leq i<n
, we have the equality
\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ\tau_{i+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=\tau_{i+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ\tau_{i+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
.

(6)

For all
1\leq i,j\leq n
with
\lvert i-j\rvert\geq 2
, we have the equality
\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ\tau_{j\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=\tau_{j\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
.

(7)

For all
1\leq i\leq n
and
0\leq j\leq n
, we have the equality

\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}=\begin{cases}\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n-1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}&if $i<j$,\\
\delta_{j-1\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}&if $i=j$,\\
\delta_{j+1\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}&if $i=j+1$,\\
\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\tau_{i-1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n-1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}&if $i>j+1$.\end{cases}

(8)

For all
1\leq i\leq n
and
0\leq j\leq n
, we have the equality

\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}=\begin{cases}\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}&if $i<j$,\\
\sigma_{j-1\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\tau_{j+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ\tau_{j\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}&if $i=j$,\\
\sigma_{j+1\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\tau_{j+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ\tau_{j+2\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}&if $i=j+1$,\\
\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}\circ\tau_{i+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}&if $i>j+1$.\end{cases}

We refer to morphisms in
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
of the form
\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}
,
\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}
,
\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
, or
(\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})^{-1}
, where
0\leq j\leq n
and
1\leq i\leq n
, as
generating morphisms of
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
.
The category
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
admits a strict monoidal structure
(\mathord{\varotimes},\langle 0\rangle)
, where the functor
\varotimes
is uniquely determined by the following conditions:

(9)

For all
m,n\geq 0
, we have the equality
\langle m\rangle\varotimes\langle n\rangle=\langle m+n\rangle
.

(10)

For all
m\geq 0
and
0\leq j\leq n
, we have the equalities
\langle m\rangle\varotimes\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}=\delta_{m+j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}m+n\vphantom{nm}}
and
\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}\varotimes\langle m\rangle=\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}m+n\vphantom{nm}}
.

(11)

For all
m\geq 0
and
0\leq j\leq n
, we have the equalities
\langle m\rangle\varotimes\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}=\sigma_{m+j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{m+n\vphantom{nm}}
and
\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}\varotimes\langle m\rangle=\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{m+n\vphantom{nm}}
.

(12)

For all
m\geq 0
and
1\leq i\leq n
, we have the equalities
\langle m\rangle\varotimes\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=\tau_{m+i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{m+n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
and
\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\varotimes\langle m\rangle=\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{m+n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
.

Definition 2.7
.

We let
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}
denote the category, with the same objects as
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
, and morphisms generated by
\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}\colon{\langle n\rangle}\to{\langle n+1\rangle}
for
0\leq j\leq n
and
\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}},(\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})^{-1}\colon{\langle n+1\rangle}\to{\langle n+1\rangle}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, subject to conditions
(1)
and
(4)
–
(7)
of Definition
2.6
.
The category
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}
admits a strict monoidal structure
(\mathord{\varotimes},\langle 0\rangle)
, where the functor
\varotimes
is uniquely determined by conditions
(9)
,
(10)
, and
(12)
of Definition
2.6
.

Remark 2.8
.

Using the equalities in
(9)
–
(12)
, we can deduce the equality
\langle n\rangle=\langle 1\rangle^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{ijmn}}
for all
n\geq 0
, as well as the equalities
\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}}=\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{nm}}\varotimes\langle n-j\rangle=\langle j\rangle\varotimes\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{nm}}\varotimes\langle n-j\rangle
and
\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}=\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{j\vphantom{nm}}\varotimes\langle n-j\rangle=\langle j\rangle\varotimes\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{0\vphantom{nm}}\varotimes\langle n-j\rangle
for all
0\leq j\leq n
, and the equality
\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{i\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\varotimes\langle n-i\rangle=\langle i-1\rangle\varotimes\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\varotimes\langle n-i\rangle
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, and those except the third one hold not only in
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
but also in the subcategory
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}
.

We will make use of the above equalities implicitly in what follows.

The category
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
(and its subcategory
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}
) are related to the notion of
crossed simplicial groups
, introduced independently by Krasauskas
[
23
]
and Fiedorowicz–Loday
[
18
]
.

Remark 2.9
.

(1)

The full subcategory
\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B}
of
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
consisting of objects
\langle n\rangle
for all
n>0
is the same as the crossed simplicial group arising from the collection
\{B_{n+1}\}_{n\geq 0}
of the braid groups.

(2)

Let
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{S})_{+}
denote the category constructed from
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
by imposing an extra condition that the equality
(\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})^{2}=\mathrm{id}_{\langle n+1\rangle}
hold for all
1\leq i\leq n
, and let
\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{S}
denote the full subcategory of
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{S})_{+}
defined similarly to
(1)
.
Then,
\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{S}
is also the same as the crossed simplicial group arising from the collection
\{{S}_{n+1}\}_{n\geq 0}
of the symmetric groups.
The category
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{S})_{+}
inherits a strict monoidal structure from that on
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
via the evident functor
{(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}}\to{(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{S})_{+}}
, which is described in a more general context in Angelini-Knoll–Merling–Péroux
[
3
, Lemma 2.25]
.

(3)

The category
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}
is isomorphic to the
category
\mathfrak{B}
of braided injections
, which was introduced by Schlichtkrull–Solberg
[
35
, Definition 2.1]
, whose presentation appeared in
[
35
, Lemma 3.4]
.

(4)

Let
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{S})_{+}
denote the (monoidal) subcategory of
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{S})_{+}
defined similarly to Definition
2.7
(which can also be constructed from
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}
by imposing the condition in
(1)
).
The subcategory
\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{S}\coloneqq\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{S}\cap(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{S})_{+}
is called the
symmetric semisimplicial category
in Banerjee
[
7
, page 9]
.

We collect a few properties relating the categories
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
and
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}
and the braid groups.

Remark 2.10
.

(1)

We have an evident strict monoidal functor
\iota\colon{(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}}\to{(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}}
.
In fact,
\iota
is a faithful functor, and we can thus regard
(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}
as a monoidal subcategory of
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
, though we will not need this observation in this paper.

(2)

For all
n\geq 0
, we have a unique group homomorphism
\rho_{n+1}\colon{B_{n+1}}\to{\mathrm{Aut}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.22083pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}}(\langle n+1\rangle)}
which satisfies that
\rho_{n+1}(g_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})=\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
In fact,
\rho_{n+1}
are isomorphisms of groups, though we will not need this observation in this paper.

(3)

Similarly to
(1)
and
(2)
, we have a strict monoidal functor
{(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{S})_{+}}\to{(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{S})_{+}}
as well as group homomorphisms
{{S}_{n+1}}\to{\mathrm{Aut}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.22083pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{S})_{+}}(\langle n+1\rangle)}
for all
n\geq 0
.

Remark 2.11
.

We claim that every morphism in
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
can be written as a composition of generating morphisms.
To prove this, let
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
be a subcategory of
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
which contains all the generating morphisms.
It suffices to prove that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
.
Since conditions
(1)
–
(8)
of Definition
2.6
still hold in the subcategory
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, we have a (unique) functor
{(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}}\to{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}
which sends every object and generating morphism to itself.
Since the composition
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}\to\mathcal{I^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
is the identity on objects and on generating morphisms, this composition is equal to the identity functor
\mathrm{id}_{(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}}
.
Thus, the inclusion functor
\mathcal{I^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
is a retraction, and is in particular full.
This finishes the proof.

2.3.
Evaluations

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be categories.
The assignments
(F,X)\mapsto F(X)
and
(\eta,X)\mapsto\eta_{X}
give a functor
\mathrm{ev}\colon{[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}]\times\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
, and given a specified object
X
in
\mathcal{C}
, precomposing
\mathrm{ev}
with the evident functor
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}]\to[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}]\times\{X\}\hookrightarrow[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}]\times\mathcal{C}
gives a functor
{[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}]}\to{\mathcal{D}}
which witnesses the evaluation at
X
.
This procedure can also be formulated in the setting of closed monoidal categories.
Recall that a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
is said to be (
left
)
closed
if the functor
(-)\varotimes X
admits a right adjoint (written
[X,-]
) for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
(see Böhm
[
11
, Definition 3.23]
).
Note that a reader can safely skip the proof of Lemma
2.13
, as it becomes an elementary fact when
\mathcal{C}
is the (huge) Cartesian closed category of categories.

Notation 2.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a closed monoidal category, and
X
and
Y
objects in
\mathcal{C}
.

(1)

We let
\eta^{X}\colon{\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}}\to{[X,(-)\varotimes X]}
and
\epsilon^{X}\colon{[X,-]\varotimes X}\to{\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}}
denote the unit and the counit of the adjunction
(-)\varotimes X\dashv[X,-]
, respectively.

(2)

Given a morphism
x\colon{I}\to{X}
in
\mathcal{C}
, we let
\mathrm{ev}_{x}
denote the composite

[X,Y]\xrightarrow{r_{[X,Y]}^{-1}}[X,Y]\varotimes I\xrightarrow{[X,Y]\varotimes x}[X,Y]\varotimes X\xrightarrow{\epsilon^{X}_{Y}}Y.

(3)

Given a morphism
f\colon{X}\to{X^{\prime}}
, we let
[f,Y]
denote the composite

[X^{\prime},Y]\xrightarrow{\eta^{X}_{[X^{\prime},Y]}}[X,[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes X]\xrightarrow{[X,[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes f]}[X,[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes X^{\prime}]\xrightarrow{[X,\epsilon^{X^{\prime}}_{Y}]}[X,Y].

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a closed monoidal category,
X
and
Y
objects in
\mathcal{C}
, and
x\colon{I}\to{X}
a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
.

(1)

For all morphisms
g\colon{Y}\to{Y^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have
g\circ\mathrm{ev}_{x}=\mathrm{ev}_{x}\circ[X,g]
.

(2)

For all morphisms
f\colon{X}\to{X^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have
\mathrm{ev}_{f\circ x}=\mathrm{ev}_{x}\circ[f,Y]
.

Proof.

(1)

This is rewritten as
g\circ\epsilon^{X}_{Y}\circ([X,Y]\varotimes x)\circ r_{[X,Y]}^{-1}=\epsilon^{X}_{Y^{\prime}}\circ([X,Y^{\prime}]\varotimes x)\circ r_{[X,Y^{\prime}]}^{-1}\circ[X,g]
, which follows from the naturality of
\epsilon^{X}\circ([X,-]\varotimes x)\circ r_{[X,-]}^{-1}
.

(2)

This equality is rewritten as

\displaystyle\epsilon^{X^{\prime}}_{Y}\circ([X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes f)\circ([X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes x)\circ r_{[X^{\prime},Y]}^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\epsilon^{X}_{Y}\circ([X,Y]\varotimes x)\circ r_{[X,Y]}^{-1}\circ[X,\epsilon^{X^{\prime}}_{Y}]\circ[X,[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes f]\circ\eta^{X}_{[X^{\prime},Y]}.

Since

\displaystyle([X,Y]\varotimes x)\circ r_{[X,Y]}^{-1}\circ[X,\epsilon^{X^{\prime}}_{Y}]\circ[X,[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes f]\circ\eta^{X}_{[X^{\prime},Y]}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=([X,\epsilon^{X^{\prime}}_{Y}]\varotimes X)\circ([X,[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes f]\varotimes X)\circ(\eta^{X}_{[X^{\prime},Y]}\varotimes X)\circ([X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes X)\circ r_{[X^{\prime},Y]}^{-1}

by the naturality of
((-)\varotimes x)\circ r^{-1}
, it suffices to prove that

\epsilon^{X^{\prime}}_{Y}\circ([X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes f)=\epsilon^{X}_{Y}\circ([X,\epsilon^{X^{\prime}}_{Y}]\varotimes X)\circ([X,[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes f]\varotimes X)\circ(\eta^{X}_{[X^{\prime},Y]}\varotimes X).

Since
\epsilon^{X}_{Y}\circ([X,\epsilon^{X^{\prime}}_{Y}]\varotimes X)\circ([X,[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes f]\varotimes X)=\epsilon^{X^{\prime}}_{Y}\circ([X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes f)\circ\epsilon^{X}_{[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes X}
by the naturality of
\epsilon^{X}
, it remains to prove that
\epsilon^{X}_{[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes X}\circ(\eta^{X}_{[X^{\prime},Y]}\varotimes X)=\mathrm{id}_{[X^{\prime},Y]\varotimes X}
, which follows from (one of) the triangle identities
(\epsilon^{X}\star((-)\varotimes X))\circ(((-)\varotimes X)\star\eta^{X})=\mathrm{id}_{(-)\varotimes X}
(see
[
25
, page 85, (9)]
).

∎

2.4.
Linear categories

Recall that a
\Bbbk
-linear category
is a category which is enriched over the category of
\Bbbk
-modules.
In other words, a
\Bbbk
-linear category is a category
\mathcal{C}
where the set
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
of morphisms from
X
to
Y
is endowed with a fixed structure of a
\Bbbk
-module (written
{\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
) for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
, such that the composition law
{{\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}}(Y,Z)\times{\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}}(X,Y)}\to{{\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{\mathcal{C}}}(X,Z)}
is
\Bbbk
-bilinear for all
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}
.
We omit the definitions of
\Bbbk
-linear functors
,
\Bbbk
-linear natural transformations
, and
\Bbbk
-linear equivalences
, which are just special cases of enriched functors, enriched transformations, and equivalences of enriched categories.
In what follows, we will not distinguish a
\Bbbk
-linear category and its
underlying category
.

The next result is standard in enriched category theory, and we omit the proof.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
F\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
be a
\Bbbk
-linear functor, and
M
an object in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then, the collection of
\Bbbk
-linear maps
{{\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{C}}(X,M)}\to{{\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{D}}(F(X),F(M))}
indexed by
X\in\mathcal{C}
induces a natural transformation
{{\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{C}}(-,M)}\to{{\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{D}}(F(-),F(M))}
, which is a natural isomorphism if
F
is fully faithful in the sense that the
\Bbbk
-linear maps
{{\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)}\to{{\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{{\hskip 0.40974pt}\mathcal{D}}(F(X),F(Y))}
are isomorphisms for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
.

3.
Braided cohomology of braided coalgebras

In this section, we begin by recalling the notion of braided coalgebras in monoidal categories in the sense of Ardizzoni–Menini
[
4
]
.
To every braided coalgebra in a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, we can associate a strict monoidal functor from
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}
(called a strict monoidal augmented braided simplicial object in our terminology), which takes values in
\mathcal{C}
when
\mathcal{C}
is strict (see Proposition
3.10
), and in
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
in the general case (see Proposition
3.13
).
We will then use this to define the notion of the braided cochain complex and the braided cohomology of a braided coalgebra in a
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category in Subsection
3.2
.
We also give a functoriality result on the braided cochain complex (see Lemma
3.21
) which will be useful later, and we provide an explicit description of an augmented braided simplicial object associated to a braided coalgebra, defined in Definition
3.22

(1)
, up to isomorphism (see Remark
3.23
).

3.1.
Braided coalgebras and monoidal augmented braided simplicial objects

We review here the notion of braided coalgebras, and define the notion of (monoidal) augmented braided (semi)simplicial objects.
Then, we associate, to every braided coalgebra
(C,c)
in a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, a strict monoidal augmented braided simplicial object
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}_{\bullet}(C,c)
in
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
(see Proposition
3.13
).

Definition 3.1

(
[
4
, Definition 2.1 (1) and (3)]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a monoidal category.

(1)

A triple
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon)
is called a
coalgebra in
\mathcal{C}
if
C
is an object in
\mathcal{C}
,
\Delta\colon{C}\to{C\varotimes C}
and
\varepsilon\colon{C}\to{I}
are morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
, and the following equalities are satisfied:

(3.1)

\displaystyle a_{C,C,C}\circ(\Delta\varotimes C)\circ\Delta

\displaystyle=(C\varotimes\Delta)\circ\Delta;

(3.2)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\varotimes C)\circ\Delta

\displaystyle=l_{C}^{-1};

\displaystyle(C\varotimes\varepsilon)\circ\Delta

\displaystyle=r_{C}^{-1}.

(2)

A pair
(C,c)
is called a
braided object in
\mathcal{C}
if
C
is an object in
\mathcal{C}
,
c\colon{C\varotimes C}\to{C\varotimes C}
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}
, and the following equality is satisfied:

(3.3)

\begin{split}&a_{C,C,C}\circ(c\varotimes C)\circ a_{C,C,C}^{-1}\circ(C\varotimes c)\circ a_{C,C,C}\circ(c\varotimes C)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=(C\varotimes c)\circ a_{C,C,C}\circ(c\varotimes C)\circ a_{C,C,C}^{-1}\circ(C\varotimes c)\circ a_{C,C,C}.\end{split}

(3)

A quadruple
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon,c)
is called a
braided coalgebra in
\mathcal{C}
if
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon)
is a coalgebra in
\mathcal{C}
,
(C,c)
is a braided object in
\mathcal{C}
, and the following equalities are satisfied:

(3.4)

\displaystyle l_{C}\circ(\varepsilon\varotimes C)\circ c

\displaystyle=r_{C}\circ(C\varotimes\varepsilon);

\displaystyle r_{C}\circ(C\varotimes\varepsilon)\circ c

\displaystyle=l_{C}\circ(\varepsilon\varotimes C);

(3.5)

\displaystyle a_{C,C,C}\circ(\Delta\varotimes C)\circ c

\displaystyle=(C\varotimes c)\circ a_{C,C,C}\circ(c\varotimes C)\circ a_{C,C,C}^{-1}\circ(C\varotimes\Delta);

\displaystyle a_{C,C,C}^{-1}\circ(C\varotimes\Delta)\circ c

\displaystyle=(c\varotimes C)\circ a_{C,C,C}^{-1}\circ(C\varotimes c)\circ a_{C,C,C}\circ(\Delta\varotimes C).

Remark 3.2
.

We refer to Baez
[
6
, page 886]
for the dual notion (called an
r
-algebra
by the author), and to Takeuchi
[
40
, Definition 5.1]
for the notion of a
braided bialgebra
.

Example 3.3
.

(\langle 1\rangle,\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{0\vphantom{nm}},\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{nm}},\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})
is a braided coalgebra in
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
Indeed, it follows from conditions
(1)
–
(3)
of Definition
2.6
that
(\langle 1\rangle,\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{0\vphantom{nm}},\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{nm}})
is a coalgebra in
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}
(see
[
25
, page 175]
for a dual statement).
We write
\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}
for the composition law in
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
It follows from condition
(4)
that
\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
is an isomorphism in
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and from conditions
(5)
and
(12)
that the equalities

\displaystyle(\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\varotimes\langle 1\rangle)\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}(\langle 1\rangle\varotimes\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}(\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\varotimes\langle 1\rangle)

\displaystyle=\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{2\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}\tau_{2\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{2\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{2\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=\tau_{2\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{2\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{2\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}\tau_{2\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{2\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}

\displaystyle=(\langle 1\rangle\varotimes\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}(\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\varotimes\langle 1\rangle)\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}(\langle 1\rangle\varotimes\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})

hold, and hence
(\langle 1\rangle,\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})
is a braided object in
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
On the other hand, (
3.4
) is verified as

(\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{nm}}\varotimes\langle 1\rangle)\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}1\vphantom{nm}}\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=\delta_{1\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}1\vphantom{nm}}=\langle 1\rangle\varotimes\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{nm}},

where the first and third equalities follow from condition
(10)
, and the second equality from condition
(7)
, whereas (
3.5
) is verified as

(\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{0\vphantom{nm}}\varotimes\langle 1\rangle)\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{1\vphantom{nm}}\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=\tau_{2\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{2\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{2\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}\sigma_{1\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{1\vphantom{nm}}=(\langle 1\rangle\varotimes\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}(\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\varotimes\langle 1\rangle)\mathbin{\circ_{\mathrm{op}}}(\langle 1\rangle\varotimes\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{0\vphantom{nm}}),

where the first and third equalities follow from conditions
(11)
and
(12)
, and the second equality from condition
(8)
.
The remaining equalities can be verified similarly.

We now explain how coalgebras in braided monoidal categories can be promoted to braided coalgebras in a natural manner.

Example 3.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},c)
be a braided monoidal category.

(1)

Let
C
be an object in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then,
(C,{c}_{C,C})
is a braided object in
\mathcal{C}
, since
{c}_{C,C}
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}
and since (
3.3
) follows from
[
22
, Theorem XIII.1.3]
.

(2)

Let
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon)
be a coalgebra in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then,
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon,{c}_{C,C})
is a braided coalgebra in
\mathcal{C}
.
Indeed, it follows from
(1)
that
(C,{c}_{C,C})
is a braided object in
\mathcal{C}
, and we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle l_{C}\circ(\varepsilon\varotimes C)\circ{c}_{C,C}

\displaystyle=l_{C}\circ{c}_{C,I}\circ(C\varotimes\varepsilon)=r_{C}\circ(C\varotimes\varepsilon);

(3.7)

\displaystyle r_{C}\circ(C\varotimes\varepsilon)\circ{c}_{C,C}

\displaystyle=r_{C}\circ{c}_{I,C}\circ(\varepsilon\varotimes C)=l_{C}\circ(\varepsilon\varotimes C);

(3.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{C,C,C}\circ(\Delta\varotimes C)\circ{c}_{C,C}&=a_{C,C,C}\circ{c}_{C,C\varotimes C}\circ(C\varotimes\Delta)\\
&=(C\varotimes{c}_{C,C})\circ a_{C,C,C}\circ({c}_{C,C}\varotimes C)\circ a_{C,C,C}^{-1}\circ(C\varotimes\Delta);\end{split}

(3.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a_{C,C,C}^{-1}\circ(C\varotimes\Delta)\circ{c}_{C,C}&=a_{C,C,C}^{-1}\circ{c}_{C\varotimes C,C}\circ(\Delta\varotimes C)\\
&=({c}_{C,C}\varotimes C)\circ a_{C,C,C}^{-1}\circ(C\varotimes{c}_{C,C})\circ a_{C,C,C}\circ(\Delta\varotimes C),\end{split}

where the first equalities follow from (
2.5
), the second equalities of (
3.6
) and (
3.7
) follow from
[
22
, Proposition XIII.1.2]
, and those of (
3.8
) and (
3.9
) follow from (
2.6
) and (
2.7
), respectively.

We will often abbreviate a braided coalgebra
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon,c)
to
(C,c)
.

In Subsection
4.3
, we will deal with more concrete examples of braided coalgebras, which arise from quasi-triangular bialgebras (see Definition
4.19
and Remark
4.20
).

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category.

(1)

An
augmented braided simplicial object in
\mathcal{C}
is a functor
{(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
.

(2)

An
augmented braided semisimplicial object in
\mathcal{C}
is a functor
{(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a monoidal category.
A
monoidal augmented braided simplicial object in
\mathcal{C}
is a monoidal functor
{(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
.
When
\mathcal{C}
is strict as a monoidal category, a monoidal augmented braided simplicial object in
\mathcal{C}
is said to be
strict
if it is strict as a monoidal functor.

A (
strict
)
monoidal augmented braided semisimplicial object
is defined similarly.

Remark 3.7
.

By precomposing with the strict monoidal functor
\iota\colon{(\bm{\Delta}_{\hskip 0.24582pt{\textnormal{inj}}}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}}\to{(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}}
of Remark
2.10

(1)
, any (monoidal) augmented braided simplicial object gives rise to a (monoidal) augmented braided semisimplicial object, and this transference preserves strictness.

Notation 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (monoidal) category.
A (monoidal) augmented braided (semi)simplicial object in
\mathcal{C}
is typically denoted by
X_{\bullet}
or
X
.
In these cases, we write
X_{n}
for its value on
\langle n\rangle
for all
n\geq 0
.

Notation 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category, and
X_{\bullet}
an augmented braided simplicial object in
\mathcal{C}
.

(1)

For all
0\leq j\leq n
, we write
d^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\coloneqq X(\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}})
, which is a morphism from
X_{n+1}
to
X_{n}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

(2)

For all
0\leq j\leq n
, we write
s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\coloneqq X(\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}})
, which is a morphism from
X_{n+1}
to
X_{n+2}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

(3)

For all
1\leq i\leq n
, we write
t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\coloneqq X(\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})
, which is a morphism from
X_{n+1}
to
X_{n+1}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

We use the symbols of Notation
3.9

(1)
and
(3)
for augmented braided semisimplicial objects as well.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a monoidal category, and
X_{\bullet}
a monoidal augmented braided simplicial object in
\mathcal{C}
.
Since
(\langle 1\rangle,\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{0\vphantom{nm}},\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{nm}},\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})
is a braided coalgebra in
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}}
by Example
3.3
, one can construct a braided coalgebra in
\mathcal{C}
using Proposition
3.12
below.
The following proposition gives a converse assignment in the case when the monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
is strict.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a strict monoidal category, and
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon,c)
a braided coalgebra in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then, there exists a unique strict monoidal augmented braided simplicial object
X_{\bullet}
in
\mathcal{C}
which satisfies the equalities
X_{1}=C
,
X(\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{nm}})=\varepsilon
,
X(\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{0\vphantom{nm}})=\Delta
, and
X(\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})=c
.

Proof.

We first prove the uniqueness.
Let
X_{\bullet}
be any strict monoidal augmented braided simplicial object in
\mathcal{C}
.
By Definition
2.6
,
X_{\bullet}
can be recovered from the collection
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
of objects in
\mathcal{C}
and the collections

\{d^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\colon{X_{n+1}}\to{X_{n}}\}_{0\leq j\leq n};\quad\{s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\colon{X_{n+1}}\to{X_{n+2}}\}_{0\leq j\leq n};\quad\{t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\colon{X_{n+1}}\to{X_{n+1}}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}

of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
.
Since
X_{n}=X(\langle 1\rangle^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{ijmn}})=(X_{1})^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{ijmn}}
for all
n\geq 0
,

\displaystyle d^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}

\displaystyle=X(\langle j\rangle\varotimes\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{nm}}\varotimes\langle n-j\rangle)=(X_{1})^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes X(\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{nm}})\varotimes(X_{1})^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}};

\displaystyle s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}

\displaystyle=X(\langle j\rangle\varotimes\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{0\vphantom{nm}}\varotimes\langle n-j\rangle)=(X_{1})^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes X(\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{0\vphantom{nm}})\varotimes(X_{1})^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}

for all
0\leq j\leq n
, and
t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}=X(\langle i-1\rangle\varotimes\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\varotimes\langle n-i\rangle)=(X_{1})^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes X(\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})\varotimes(X_{1})^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, it follows that
X_{\bullet}
is uniquely determined from the quadruple
(X_{1},X(\delta_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{nm}}),X(\sigma_{0\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{0\vphantom{nm}}),X(\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}))
.
This proves the uniqueness.
Next, we prove the existence.
We define collections

\{d^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\colon{C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}}\to{C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{ijmn}}}\}_{0\leq j\leq n};\quad\{s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\colon{C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}}\to{C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n+2)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}}\}_{0\leq j\leq n};\quad\{t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\colon{C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}}\to{C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}

of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
as follows:

(3.10)

d^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\coloneqq C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}};\quad s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\coloneqq C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\Delta\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}};\quad t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\coloneqq C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}.

To define an augmented braided simplicial object in
\mathcal{C}
from these collections of morphisms and from the collection
\{C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{ijmn}}\}_{n\geq 0}
of objects, it suffices to prove that these collections satisfy the (dual) conditions of
(1)
through
(8)
of Definition
2.6
.
The condition
(1)
is verified as follows:

\displaystyle d^{\hskip 0.57367pti\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n-1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ d^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}

\displaystyle=(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes((\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(j-i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes I)\circ(C\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(j-i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes\varepsilon))\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes((I\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(j-i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes\varepsilon)\circ(\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(j-i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes C))\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}

\displaystyle=(C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(j-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})=d^{\hskip 0.81949ptj-1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n-1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ d^{\hskip 0.57367pti\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}.

We note that condition
(2)
in the case
i<j
, conditions
(3)
,
(7)
, and
(8)
in the case
i<j
or
i>j+1
, and condition
(5)
follow directly from (
3.10
) as well.
The condition
(2)
in the case
i=j
is verified as

\displaystyle s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}

\displaystyle=(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\Delta\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\Delta\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes((\Delta\varotimes C)\circ\Delta)\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes((C\varotimes\Delta)\circ\Delta)\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}

\displaystyle=(C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(j+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes\Delta\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\Delta\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})=s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}},

where the third equality follows from (
3.1
).
The condition
(3)
in the case
i=j
is verified as

\displaystyle d^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}

\displaystyle=(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\Delta\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes((\varepsilon\varotimes C)\circ\Delta)\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\mathrm{id}_{C}\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=\mathrm{id}_{C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.22083pt(n+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}},

where the third equality follows from (
3.2
).
The condition
(4)
follows since
c
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}
.
The condition
(6)
is verified as

\displaystyle t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}

\displaystyle=(C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes((c\varotimes C)\circ(C\varotimes c)\circ(c\varotimes C))\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes((C\varotimes c)\circ(c\varotimes C)\circ(C\varotimes c))\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}

\displaystyle=(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})

\displaystyle=t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}i+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}},

where the third equality follows from (
3.3
).
The condition
(7)
in the case
i=j+1
is verified as

\displaystyle d^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ t^{\hskip 0.81949ptj+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}

\displaystyle=(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes((\varepsilon\varotimes C)\circ c)\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes(C\varotimes\varepsilon)\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(j+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=d^{\hskip 0.81949ptj+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}},

where the third equality follows from (
3.4
).
The condition
(8)
in the case
i=j+1
is verified as

\displaystyle s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ t^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}j+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}

\displaystyle=(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\Delta\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes((\Delta\varotimes C)\circ c)\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes((C\varotimes c)\circ(c\varotimes C)\circ(C\varotimes\Delta))\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}

\displaystyle=(C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(j+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})\circ(C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(j+1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes\Delta\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}})

\displaystyle=t^{\hskip 0.81949ptj+2\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ t^{\hskip 0.81949ptj+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n+1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}\circ s^{\hskip 0.81949ptj+1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}_{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}},

where the third equality follows from (
3.5
).
The remaining conditions follow from similar arguments, using the unlabeled equalities of Definition
3.1
.
We thus obtain an augmented braided simplicial object
X_{\bullet}
in
\mathcal{C}
which satisfies the displayed equalities.
It remains to show that the functor
X_{\bullet}
is strict monoidal.
We have
X_{m}\varotimes X_{n}=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}m\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{ijmn}}=C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(m+n)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=X_{m+n}
for all
m,n\geq 0
and
X_{0}=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}0\vphantom{ijmn}}=I
by definition.
Note that the set
\{(\phi,\phi^{\prime})\mid X(\phi)\varotimes X(\phi^{\prime})=X(\phi\varotimes\phi^{\prime})\}
contains all the pairs of the identity morphisms, and is closed under pointwise composition.
By Remark
2.11
, it is enough to verify that
X_{m}\varotimes X(\phi)=X(\langle m\rangle\varotimes\phi)
and
X(\phi)\varotimes X_{m}=X(\phi\varotimes\langle m\rangle)
hold for all
m\geq 0
and all the generating morphisms
\phi
of
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
.
We only prove the former equality; the latter can be proven similarly.
For
0\leq j\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle X_{m}\varotimes X(\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}})

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}m\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\Delta\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(m+j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes\Delta\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=X(\delta_{m+j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}m+n\vphantom{nm}})=X(\langle m\rangle\varotimes\delta_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{{\hskip 0.40974pt}n\vphantom{nm}});

\displaystyle X_{m}\varotimes X(\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}})

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}m\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}j\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(m+j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes\varepsilon\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-j)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=X(\sigma_{m+j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{m+n\vphantom{nm}})=X(\langle m\rangle\varotimes\sigma_{j\vphantom{ij+1m}}^{n\vphantom{nm}}),

where we used conditions
(10)
and
(11)
of Definition
2.6
in the fourth equality, and for
1\leq i\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle X_{m}\varotimes X(\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})

\displaystyle=C^{\varotimes{\hskip 0.40974pt}m\vphantom{ijmn}}\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(m+i-1)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}\varotimes c\varotimes C^{\varotimes\hskip 0.24582pt(n-i)\vphantom{ijmn-+12}}=X(\tau_{m+i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{m+n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})=X(\langle m\rangle\varotimes\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}),

where we used condition
(12)
of Definition
2.6
in the fourth equality.
The case
\phi=(\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}})^{-1}
can be verified similarly, and it can also be deduced from the case
\phi=\tau_{i\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{n\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
, since the set mentioned above is closed under taking pointwise inverses.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a strict monoidal category.
The assignment
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon,c)\mapsto X_{\bullet}
of Proposition
3.10
induces a bijective correspondence between braided coalgebras in
\mathcal{C}
and strict monoidal augmented braided simplicial objects in
\mathcal{C}
.
We note that
(\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}
is equipped with a unique braiding
b
such that
b_{\langle 1\rangle,\langle 1\rangle}=\tau_{1\vphantom{ij+1-NoValue-}}^{1\vphantom{n-NoValue-}}
(see
[
2
, Definition 4.2.1]
).
Given a braiding
c
on
\mathcal{C}
, we obtain as a restriction a bijective correspondence between braided coalgebras of the form
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon,{c}_{C,C})
in
\mathcal{C}
and strict braided monoidal functors from
((\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}},b)
to
(\mathcal{C},c)
, and the former objects can be identified with coalgebras in
\mathcal{C}
.
It follows that strict braided monoidal functors from
((\bm{\Delta}\mathbf{B})_{+}^{\mathrm{op}},b)
to
(\mathcal{C},c)
correspond bijectively to coalgebras in
\mathcal{C}
.
This fact appears, for example, in
[
2
, Observation 4.2.2]
.

Let
\imath\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}
a monoidal functor, and suppose that
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
is strict.
Given a braided coalgebra
(C,c)
in
\mathcal{C}
, one might attempt to construct a monoidal augmented braided simplicial object in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, either by

(a)

passing
(C,c)
to a braided coalgebra in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
via
\imath
, and then using Proposition
3.10
, or

(b)

formulating Proposition
3.10
in the non-strict setting to pass
(C,c)
to a (possibly non-strict) monoidal augmented braided simplicial object in
\mathcal{C}
, and then postcomposing it with
\imath
.

As it would be tedious to take the second approach, we prove that a monoidal functor transfers braided coalgebras living in different monoidal categories.

Proposition 3.12

(
[
4
, Proposition 2.5]
)
.

Let
(F,\varphi_{2},\varphi_{0})\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
be a monoidal functor, and
(C,\Delta,\varepsilon,c)
a braided coalgebra in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then,
(F(C),\Delta^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime},c^{\prime})
is a braided coalgebra in
\mathcal{D}
, where we set
